- Give and Take — Adam Grant
50. Q: How can the giver approach be applied to leadership and management decision-making?
A: Show answer
Leaders can apply the giver approach by making knowledge more accessible, supporting employee development, sharing credit, encouraging constructive feedback, and helping teams obtain resources they need to succeed. A manager should not treat employees merely as instruments for achieving personal targets. Instead, the manager should identify opportunities to develop people's abilities and improve cooperation across organizational boundaries. Giving leadership does not mean avoiding difficult decisions or accepting poor performance. Leaders must still establish expectations, evaluate results, resolve conflicts, and enforce accountability. The distinguishing feature is that authority is exercised in a way that creates sustainable value for the organization and its members. Such leadership can strengthen trust, encourage participation, and create an environment in which individuals are more willing to contribute their knowledge and expertise.

Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a leadership decision according to its effect on individual development, collective performance, accountability, and organizational trust."
Key point:
- Giving leadership develops people while maintaining responsibility for organizational results.
- Sustainable leadership combines contribution, fairness, and accountability.
Source links:
- In the Company of Givers and Takers — Harvard Business Review
- Give, Take, or Match? Styles of Reciprocity, Job Satisfaction, and Work Motivation — Belle and colleagues
51. Q: What are the main lessons from Adam Rifkin's networking approach, and how can they be translated into everyday professional behavior?
A: Show answer
Adam Rifkin illustrates how professional relationships can develop through consistent small contributions rather than deliberate attempts to accumulate influential contacts. His approach includes offering advice, introducing people, supporting entrepreneurs, sharing expertise, and reconnecting with individuals who previously benefited from his assistance. The five-minute favor provides a practical expression of this philosophy by making generosity manageable within everyday activities. Another important lesson is that people should not assume they can predict which contact will become influential or provide valuable information in the future. Weak ties and dormant ties can become important sources of opportunities through circumstances that were impossible to anticipate. Professionals can apply these ideas by helping within their capacity, maintaining relationships, supporting people across different levels of influence, and encouraging recipients to assist others. The ultimate objective is to develop a network that creates opportunities for many participants rather than merely maximizing one person's access to resources.

Study instruction:
- "Prepare a personal networking action plan based on small favors, dormant-tie reconnection, professional introductions, and paying assistance forward."
Key point:
- Consistent generosity can create lasting professional relationships and broader opportunities.
- A strong network is a community of contribution rather than simply a collection of contacts.
Source links:
- Give and Take — Adam Grant
- Dormant Ties: The Value of Reconnecting — Levin, Walter, and Murnighan

Table 3
Collaborative Success, Giving, and Shared Credit
1. Q: How do giving and taking behaviors influence collaboration and long-term professional success?
A: Show answer
Collaboration is influenced not only by individual intelligence, experience, or creativity but also by how people exchange knowledge, assistance, recognition, and opportunities. Givers contribute resources and help others succeed, takers prioritize obtaining benefits for themselves, and matchers seek a balance between giving and receiving. These behaviors influence trust, information sharing, team relationships, innovation, and professional reputation. The chapter illustrates this through George Meyer, whose contributions to The Simpsons extended beyond his own writing to improving the capabilities of colleagues. His influence demonstrates that individual success can produce additional benefits when it strengthens the people working alongside the individual. Conversely, people who repeatedly claim disproportionate credit or withhold assistance can damage the relationships on which their performance depends. The central lesson is that professional achievement should be assessed through both individual contributions and the positive or negative effects those contributions create for other people.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how reciprocity styles influence individual achievement, team effectiveness, professional reputation, and long-term collaboration."
Key point:
- Collaborative success depends on both individual contribution and the ability to strengthen others.
- Giving can generate benefits that extend beyond the original contributor.
2. Q: What is the difference between givers, takers, and matchers in professional relationships?
A: Show answer
Givers primarily consider what they can contribute to other people, including sharing knowledge, providing assistance, introducing contacts, and improving collective performance. Takers prioritize obtaining benefits and may view professional relationships as opportunities to advance their own position. Matchers operate according to an exchange principle: they help others while expecting an appropriate return, and they are attentive to whether relationships remain fair. These categories describe patterns of interaction rather than permanent personality types. The same individual may behave differently depending on the relationship, organizational culture, incentives, and circumstances. A giver can establish boundaries when assistance becomes unreasonable, while a matcher can become generous when a relationship is based on trust. Understanding these distinctions helps managers recognize why some employees encourage collaboration while others create competition, dependency, or reluctance to share information.
Study instruction:
- "Classify practical workplace behaviors as giving, taking, or matching and explain the consequences of each behavior."
Key point:
- Reciprocity styles describe how people approach giving and receiving benefits.
- Actual behavior should be assessed through repeated actions rather than labels alone.
3. Q: What does it mean to become a genius maker rather than simply being an individual genius?
A: Show answer
An individual genius produces valuable ideas or results through personal expertise. A genius maker improves the ability of other people to produce valuable results. This can happen through coaching, constructive criticism, sharing information, encouraging participation, identifying overlooked capabilities, and helping colleagues overcome difficult problems. The distinction is important because some talented individuals increase their influence by making themselves indispensable, while others increase their influence by developing capable colleagues. George Meyer's contribution to The Simpsons demonstrates the second approach. He strengthened scripts, supported inexperienced writers, and contributed ideas without insisting that every contribution be attributed to him. A genius maker does not necessarily perform less demanding work or abandon personal standards. Instead, the person uses expertise to improve both individual and collective performance. In organizations, this approach supports knowledge transfer, leadership development, succession planning, and stronger institutional capabilities.
Study instruction:
- "Identify five specific management behaviors that develop the capabilities of colleagues instead of creating dependence on one expert."
Key point:
- Individual expertise becomes more valuable when it increases the capabilities of others.
- Developing other people's performance creates sustainable organizational capacity.
4. Q: Are highly creative people necessarily more generous and cooperative than less creative people?
A: Show answer
Creativity and generosity are separate characteristics. The research discussed in the chapter describes studies in which highly creative architects and scientists sometimes displayed more demanding, aggressive, or self-centered characteristics than comparison groups. Such findings suggest that creative individuals may possess confidence, independence, and willingness to challenge conventional thinking without necessarily displaying cooperative behavior. However, this does not establish that all creative people are takers or that generosity produces creativity automatically. George Meyer illustrates how strong creative independence can coexist with generosity, collaboration, and concern for others. Organizations should therefore distinguish between the ability to generate original ideas and the ability to work constructively with people. Creative performance should be evaluated through the quality of ideas, their implementation, and the conditions under which colleagues can contribute effectively.
Study instruction:
- "Distinguish creative ability from interpersonal behavior and explain why neither should be treated as a substitute for the other."
Key point:
- Creativity does not automatically demonstrate cooperation or generosity.
- Effective organizations need both original thinking and constructive collaboration.
5. Q: Why can an apparently individual achievement depend heavily on other people's contributions?
A: Show answer
Many achievements that appear individual are supported by networks of relationships, information, resources, and coordinated work. A specialist may possess exceptional technical knowledge but still rely on colleagues who understand internal systems, provide supporting information, coordinate activities, or solve operational problems. The chapter illustrates this through cardiac surgeons and investment analysts. The performance of cardiac surgeons improved with familiarity with the specific hospital teams and resources they worked with. Similarly, highly ranked investment analysts experienced performance difficulties after moving to different firms without their established colleagues. These examples demonstrate that expertise alone does not always explain performance. The institutional environment and collaborative relationships can become important components of an individual's productive capacity. Managers should therefore investigate the contribution of teams and infrastructure before attributing success entirely to one person.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze an apparently individual professional achievement and identify the people, resources, relationships, and organizational capabilities supporting it."
Key point:
- Individual performance is often partly dependent on collective resources.
- Performance attribution should consider the surrounding team and working environment.
Source links:
- The Firm Specificity of Individual Performance — Management Science
- Can They Take It With Them? — Management Science
6. Q: What is the difference between independence and interdependence in professional work?
A: Show answer
Independence refers to performing work with limited reliance on other people. Interdependence recognizes that people contribute different capabilities and require cooperation to achieve results. Independence is valuable when tasks require personal accountability, specialized judgment, or uninterrupted concentration. Interdependence becomes particularly valuable when work involves complex coordination, complementary expertise, shared resources, and multiple stages of implementation. The chapter contrasts Frank Lloyd Wright's attempt to work in isolation with the benefits he later obtained from working with apprentices and collaborators. The lesson is not that independent work is inherently ineffective. It is that refusing necessary assistance can limit productivity and reduce access to complementary knowledge. Successful professionals recognize when independent execution is appropriate and when collective resources are necessary.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an individual task with a cross-functional project and identify where independent work ends and interdependence becomes necessary."
Key point:
- Independence supports personal execution, while interdependence combines complementary capabilities.
- Refusing useful collaboration can limit otherwise strong individual performance.
7. Q: Why may a highly talented employee experience declining performance after joining another organization?
A: Show answer
Professional performance may depend on organizational relationships, established routines, specialized information, and familiarity with colleagues. When an employee changes organizations, personal technical knowledge may remain intact, but the surrounding conditions supporting effective performance may disappear. A new employee may need to understand unfamiliar systems, develop trust, learn communication habits, and establish working relationships with new colleagues. The chapter describes research on star investment analysts showing that performance could decline after moving to another firm, particularly when individuals moved without their established teams. This indicates that managers should not assume a successful employee will reproduce previous results automatically. Recruitment assessments should examine the employee's portable expertise and the organizational conditions required to use that expertise effectively.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a recruitment decision by separating the candidate's personal capabilities from the team resources and organizational relationships supporting previous performance."
Key point:
- Previous performance does not guarantee identical performance in a different organizational environment.
- Successful recruitment requires attention to team compatibility and supporting resources.
Source links:
- Can They Take It With Them? — Management Science
8. Q: Why can moving with an established team preserve professional performance better than moving alone?
A: Show answer
An established team may possess shared knowledge, communication routines, mutual trust, complementary expertise, and an understanding of how to solve problems together. Moving with colleagues can preserve some of this relationship-based capability. The chapter describes research showing that star analysts who moved with team members were more likely to maintain their performance than those who moved alone. However, team continuity does not guarantee success. The new organization must still provide adequate resources, appropriate responsibilities, and an environment that supports effective work. Organizations recruiting an established team should therefore assess whether existing relationships and capabilities can be maintained without creating organizational silos or excluding other employees. The practical objective is to preserve productive collaboration while integrating the team into its new environment.
Study instruction:
- "Compare the risks of recruiting one senior employee with recruiting an established project team, including integration and knowledge-transfer requirements."
Key point:
- Established working relationships can be an important performance resource.
- Team continuity must be accompanied by effective organizational integration.
Source links:
- Can They Take It With Them? — Management Science
9. Q: What is relationship-based knowledge, and why is it difficult to transfer between organizations?
A: Show answer
Relationship-based knowledge includes informal understandings developed through repeated interaction. Employees learn who possesses particular expertise, how colleagues interpret information, which communication methods work, where operational problems usually occur, and how decisions are actually implemented. Much of this knowledge is not fully documented in manuals or formal procedures. When employees move to another organization, they may retain technical expertise but lose access to established relationships that previously helped them perform effectively. Organizations should therefore document important operational knowledge, introduce new employees to relevant stakeholders, establish mentoring arrangements, and encourage collaborative assignments. Documentation alone cannot replace trust and familiarity, but it can reduce dependence on individual memory and make knowledge transfer more reliable.
Study instruction:
- "Identify tacit knowledge within a team and design a practical handover process that combines documentation, mentoring, and direct collaboration."
Key point:
- Professional capability includes knowledge embedded in working relationships.
- Good handovers transfer both documented procedures and relationship knowledge.
Source links:
- The Firm Specificity of Individual Performance — Management Science
10. Q: What is expedition behavior, and how can it be applied to ordinary organizational work?
A: Show answer
Expedition behavior is a collaborative approach that places the collective mission alongside individual responsibilities. It involves supporting the group's objectives, treating colleagues with respect, contributing fairly, remaining accountable, helping others, and addressing problems constructively. The concept originated in outdoor leadership and is useful because expeditions require people to coordinate under changing conditions where individual success depends on group functioning. In an office environment, expedition behavior may involve assisting another department during a critical deadline, accepting an inconvenient assignment, sharing important information, or resolving a problem before it affects the wider team. It does not mean allowing individuals to avoid responsibility or permanently transferring their workload to others. The emphasis is on contributing to the mission while maintaining accountability for one's own responsibilities.
Study instruction:
- "Apply the principles of expedition behavior to a team project and identify behaviors that support collective objectives without removing individual accountability."
Key point:
- The group's mission and individual responsibilities should reinforce each other.
- Helping colleagues should strengthen the team rather than create permanent dependency.
Source links:
- Expedition Behavior — National Outdoor Leadership School
11. Q: Why can taking responsibility for unpopular assignments strengthen a person's influence within a team?
A: Show answer
Unpopular assignments often reveal whether an employee is concerned only with personal recognition or genuinely interested in achieving collective objectives. When an employee volunteers for a necessary task that others avoid, colleagues may recognize the person's reliability and commitment. George Meyer demonstrated this by helping develop material for less prominent Saturday Night Live guests and taking responsibility for extensive rewriting rather than competing exclusively for the most visible first-draft assignments. This gave him opportunities to demonstrate his creative ability while supporting the broader production. However, managers must distinguish constructive contribution from the unfair allocation of undesirable work. Repeatedly assigning difficult or low-visibility tasks to generous employees can lead to burnout and unfair recognition. Appropriate task allocation should consider workload, expertise, development opportunities, and organizational needs.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three important but unpopular responsibilities within a team and explain how their fair allocation can improve collaboration."
Key point:
- Supporting neglected tasks demonstrates commitment to collective performance.
- Managers must recognize and distribute less desirable assignments fairly.
Source links:
- Expedition Behavior — National Outdoor Leadership School
12. Q: How does giving contribute to the expansion of collective opportunities rather than merely distributing existing benefits?
A: Show answer
Giving can expand the total value available to a group when assistance enables people to produce additional results. Sharing an idea, introducing a useful contact, improving another person's work, or transferring specialized knowledge may create opportunities that would not exist without collaboration. This is different from distributing a fixed quantity of rewards among existing participants. George Meyer's support for other writers helped improve the overall quality of creative production while also strengthening his own professional relationships. The benefits could extend to contributors who later obtained opportunities through their association with Army Man and The Simpsons. The principle is that cooperative actions can increase the value generated by the group. Managers should therefore evaluate whether collaboration creates additional capabilities, opportunities, and results rather than focusing exclusively on the immediate distribution of recognition or rewards.
Study instruction:
- "Describe a workplace situation in which knowledge sharing or assistance can increase the total value produced by the team."
Key point:
- Productive collaboration can expand opportunities for several participants simultaneously.
- Giving creates value when it improves collective capabilities or outcomes.
13. Q: Why can generous behavior reduce jealousy and resistance toward highly talented colleagues?
A: Show answer
Highly talented employees may unintentionally create insecurity when their achievements make colleagues feel inferior or threatened. Others may interpret visible success as competition for promotions, recognition, influence, or opportunities. Generous behavior changes the social meaning of talent by demonstrating that the individual's capabilities also benefit colleagues. A technically excellent employee who shares expertise, provides constructive feedback, and supports team development is less likely to be perceived solely as a competitor. The chapter explains how George Meyer demonstrated his creative abilities through contributions that improved collective work rather than pursuing every opportunity to outperform his colleagues publicly. This approach can reduce unnecessary competition, although it cannot eliminate every disagreement or organizational rivalry. Fair performance management and transparent recognition remain necessary.
Study instruction:
- "Compare how colleagues may respond to a talented employee who competes for personal recognition with one who actively develops team capabilities."
Key point:
- Sharing expertise can reduce perceptions of talent as a threat.
- Generosity must be supported by fair organizational recognition and opportunities.
14. Q: What are idiosyncrasy credits, and how can they influence professional credibility?
A: Show answer
Idiosyncrasy credits are a social influence concept describing the accumulated acceptance or goodwill that allows a group member greater freedom to depart from established expectations. A person who consistently contributes, performs reliably, and demonstrates concern for group objectives may earn greater trust from colleagues. This credibility can make people more receptive when the individual later proposes an unconventional idea or challenges existing procedures. In the chapter, George Meyer developed credibility through repeated contributions to The Simpsons. Consequently, colleagues became more willing to consider unusual creative suggestions from him. Idiosyncrasy credits are not a formal entitlement or an unlimited license to ignore organizational standards. They depend on the group's perceptions and may decrease when behavior becomes unfair, unreliable, or inconsistent with collective interests.
Study instruction:
- "Explain how repeated constructive contributions can increase professional credibility and identify behaviors that may damage that credibility."
Key point:
- Consistent contributions can build trust that supports future influence.
- Credibility is conditional and can be lost through unreliable or self-serving behavior.
Source links:
- Conformity, Status, and Idiosyncrasy Credit — Psychological Review
15. Q: Why are colleagues more willing to consider unconventional suggestions from people with a strong reputation for helping others?
A: Show answer
An unconventional suggestion can create uncertainty because colleagues may question the motivation behind it. If a person has a history of supporting collective objectives, others may be more likely to interpret the proposal as an attempt to improve the organization rather than an attempt to obtain personal advantage. The chapter describes how George Meyer's credibility helped colleagues consider creative suggestions that they might otherwise have viewed as unusual or disruptive. This does not mean that generous employees should automatically receive approval. Their suggestions still require examination for quality, feasibility, cost, and risk. The relevant distinction concerns how trust influences the willingness to listen, discuss, and evaluate a proposal before making a decision.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare an example of an unconventional process-improvement proposal and explain how evidence, professional credibility, and stakeholder interests affect its acceptance."
Key point:
- Trust can make constructive challenges easier to communicate.
- Credibility increases consideration, not automatic approval.
Source links:
- Conformity, Status, and Idiosyncrasy Credit — Psychological Review
16. Q: What is the difference between sharing credit and allowing colleagues to take credit for work they did not perform?
A: Show answer
Sharing credit means recognizing the contributions of people who genuinely participated in producing a result. Allowing inaccurate attribution occurs when recognition is assigned to individuals who did not make the stated contribution or when important contributors are deliberately excluded. These situations are not equivalent. Collaborative work often produces shared outcomes in which ideas, feedback, revisions, and implementation contributions are difficult to separate completely. Recognition should reflect the actual nature of those contributions without requiring every small suggestion to be individually documented in public. However, significant contributions must not be appropriated or deliberately misrepresented. George Meyer voluntarily shared ideas without pursuing every individual writing credit, but this was different from another person falsely claiming his work as their own. Professional credit-sharing should preserve accuracy, fairness, and accountability.
Study instruction:
- "Review a collaborative project and distinguish shared authorship, supporting contributions, individual ownership, and inaccurate attribution."
Key point:
- Giving credit does not mean ignoring the identity of actual contributors.
- Fair recognition combines generosity with truthful attribution.
17. Q: Why should professional recognition not always be treated as a zero-sum resource?
A: Show answer
A zero-sum perspective assumes that one person's recognition necessarily reduces the recognition available to another. This assumption can encourage employees to protect ideas, exaggerate personal contributions, and avoid acknowledging collaborators. In many collaborative activities, several people can receive legitimate recognition for different contributions to the same achievement. Recognition can also reinforce trust and encourage future knowledge sharing. George Meyer expressed this principle through his willingness to acknowledge the contributions of other writers while remaining confident that his own work had value. Nevertheless, organizations must establish transparent recognition criteria because some rewards, such as a single promotion or a limited award, genuinely involve competition. Managers should distinguish between competitive reward allocation and the broader opportunity to recognize multiple contributors fairly.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a collaborative achievement and design an attribution approach that recognizes individual and collective contributions accurately."
Key point:
- Recognition can acknowledge several genuine contributors to one achievement.
- Limited rewards still require transparent and fair allocation criteria.
18. Q: What are the long-term consequences of consistently claiming disproportionate credit for collective achievements?
A: Show answer
Claiming disproportionate credit can damage relationships because colleagues may feel that their effort, expertise, and loyalty have been ignored. The immediate benefit may be increased visibility or recognition, but the longer-term consequences can include reduced trust, limited information sharing, staff turnover, and fewer opportunities for collaboration. The chapter describes Jonas Salk's failure to acknowledge important scientific colleagues during the announcement of the polio vaccine. Some members of his research team felt deeply excluded, and the episode damaged professional relationships. The wider lesson is that technical achievement does not automatically justify sole ownership of collaborative results. Managers should establish recognition practices that acknowledge significant contributors and maintain accurate records of individual responsibilities.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze a project in which contributors were excluded from recognition and identify the effects on morale, retention, trust, and future collaboration."
Key point:
- Disproportionate credit claims can create long-term relationship costs.
- Collaborative achievements require accurate recognition of meaningful contributions.
19. Q: How does knowledge hiding damage collaboration and creative performance?
A: Show answer
Knowledge hiding occurs when a person intentionally withholds information requested by another person. It differs from simply lacking information, being unable to share confidential material, or not having enough time to respond. When employees repeatedly hide knowledge, colleagues may interpret the behavior as uncooperative and respond by withholding information themselves. This can produce a cycle of distrust that reduces access to ideas, expertise, and practical assistance. Research discussed in the chapter connects knowledge hiding with reduced creativity through reciprocal relationship effects. Organizations should establish clear rules for legitimate confidentiality while encouraging appropriate information sharing. Managers can reduce unnecessary knowledge hiding by recognizing helpful behavior, creating accessible knowledge repositories, and making collaboration part of performance expectations.
Study instruction:
- "Identify three examples of legitimate confidentiality and three examples of harmful knowledge hiding in a workplace."
Key point:
- Intentional knowledge hiding can create reciprocal distrust.
- Information-sharing policies must balance collaboration with legitimate confidentiality requirements.
Source links:
- What Goes Around Comes Around: Knowledge Hiding, Perceived Motivational Climate, and Creativity — Academy of Management Journal
20. Q: What is the responsibility bias, and why does it occur in collaborative relationships?
A: Show answer
Responsibility bias is the tendency to overestimate one's own contribution relative to the contributions of other people. It can occur in marriages, professional partnerships, project teams, research groups, and management relationships. People are generally more aware of their own work because they directly experience the time, difficulty, and decisions involved. They may observe only part of what other contributors do. Consequently, individuals can sincerely believe that they performed more than their fair share even when other participants have similarly strong perceptions. The chapter discusses research by Michael Ross and Fiore Sicoly showing that people frequently overestimate their contribution to shared results. This bias can create disagreements about effort, recognition, workload distribution, and responsibility for failures.
Study instruction:
- "Explain the responsibility bias and identify how it can create conflict in project teams."
Key point:
- Individuals often have more complete information about their own efforts than other people's efforts.
- Contribution disputes may arise from genuine differences in perception rather than deliberate dishonesty.
Source links:
- Egocentric Biases in Availability and Attribution — Journal of Personality and Social Psychology
21. Q: How does information discrepancy contribute to unfair judgments about individual contributions?
A: Show answer
Information discrepancy occurs when people have unequal access to information about each other's efforts. Employees can easily remember their own meetings, research, problem-solving activities, and difficult assignments, but they may not observe comparable activities performed by colleagues. This creates an incomplete basis for comparing contributions. For example, a project manager may remember preparing the reporting framework but fail to recognize the hours that a finance officer spent validating supporting documentation. Both people may sincerely believe that their work was the most important. To reduce this problem, organizations should make task responsibilities and contributions more visible. Shared progress records, documented deliverables, regular discussions, and clear role definitions provide a more balanced basis for evaluating performance.
Study instruction:
- "Map the visible and less-visible contributions of team members in a shared project and identify information gaps affecting performance evaluation."
Key point:
- Unobserved contributions are easily underestimated.
- Shared work records provide a stronger basis for recognizing contributions.
Source links:
- Egocentric Biases in Availability and Attribution — Journal of Personality and Social Psychology
22. Q: How can managers reduce responsibility bias when evaluating team contributions?
A: Show answer
Managers can improve contribution assessments by asking employees to identify what colleagues contributed before estimating their own contribution. This simple change encourages people to consider work that is less visible to them. Managers should also use documented responsibilities, project milestones, deliverables, peer feedback, and evidence of problem-solving activities. For important assignments, a contribution register can record who developed the initial idea, reviewed it, supplied information, implemented changes, and approved the final work. Such records should recognize substantive contributions rather than create excessive administrative requirements. Independent review is useful when disagreements affect performance assessments or promotion decisions. The objective is to establish a balanced understanding of the work rather than reward the person who communicates their own contribution most persuasively.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a team contribution assessment that records other people's contributions before evaluating the individual's own work."
Key point:
- Considering colleagues' contributions first can reduce self-focused estimates.
- Contribution assessments should combine individual accounts with verifiable evidence.
Source links:
- Egocentric Biases in Availability and Attribution — Journal of Personality and Social Psychology
23. Q: How should a team allocate responsibility when a project succeeds or fails?
A: Show answer
A team should examine the actual decision-making process, assigned responsibilities, evidence, dependencies, and individual actions before allocating responsibility. When a project succeeds, members should recognize the people whose contributions made the result possible. When it fails, the team should identify the decisions, omissions, and external conditions that contributed to the outcome. The chapter describes differences between givers and takers in how they allocate credit and blame: takers were more likely to claim credit for success and blame partners for failure, while givers were more likely to recognize others' contributions and accept responsibility for problems. This does not mean that a giver should accept blame for events outside their control. Accountability must remain evidence-based and fair. The purpose is to support learning rather than protect individual reputations at the expense of the group.
Study instruction:
- "Conduct a post-project review that separates individual accountability, team dependencies, external factors, successes, and corrective actions."
Key point:
- Success and failure should be evaluated using the same evidence-based fairness.
- Accountability should encourage learning rather than scapegoating or self-promotion.
Source links:
- Egocentric Biases in Availability and Attribution — Journal of Personality and Social Psychology
24. Q: What is psychological safety in a collaborative team?
A: Show answer
Psychological safety is a shared belief that members of a team can take interpersonal risks without being humiliated, punished, or unfairly penalized. It allows employees to ask questions, acknowledge mistakes, challenge assumptions, propose unusual ideas, and request assistance. The chapter describes how George Meyer helped create an environment in which writers felt able to present ideas even when they were inexperienced or uncertain. Psychological safety is particularly valuable in creative and knowledge-based work because learning often requires experimentation and discussion of unsuccessful approaches. Research by Amy Edmondson connects team psychological safety with learning behavior and team performance. It is important to recognize that psychological safety is not the absence of responsibility or professional standards. It provides the conditions for constructive participation and honest discussion.
Study instruction:
- "Evaluate a team environment using examples of speaking up, asking for help, reporting mistakes, questioning decisions, and proposing new ideas."
Key point:
- Psychological safety supports interpersonal risk-taking and learning.
- Teams need both open communication and clear performance expectations.
Source links:
- Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Administrative Science Quarterly
25. Q: Does psychological safety mean employees should never experience criticism or disagreement?
A: Show answer
Psychological safety does not mean that every idea must be accepted or that employees should be protected from legitimate criticism. It means that people can participate in discussions without fearing humiliation or personal attacks. High standards remain necessary, particularly when decisions affect financial accuracy, compliance, service quality, or organizational reputation. A psychologically safe team can challenge weak proposals directly while treating the people who make them with respect. For example, a finance manager can identify an unsupported reconciliation adjustment and require correction without describing the employee as incompetent. This distinction encourages accurate reporting because employees are more likely to disclose mistakes early. The objective is respectful challenge, clear accountability, and continuous improvement rather than artificial agreement.
Study instruction:
- "Rewrite three examples of personal criticism as specific, evidence-based feedback that maintains professional accountability."
Key point:
- Psychological safety and high standards can coexist.
- Criticize the work or decision without humiliating the individual.
Source links:
- Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Administrative Science Quarterly
26. Q: How can managers provide direct criticism without discouraging future participation?
A: Show answer
Effective feedback identifies the specific issue, explains its impact, and provides a practical direction for improvement. Managers should distinguish the quality of the work from the personal worth or ability of the individual. For example, instead of saying that an employee is careless, a reviewer can explain that a particular reconciliation omitted two bank transactions and describe the verification required. Feedback should be timely, specific, and appropriate to the seriousness of the issue. When work contains valuable elements, managers can identify what should be retained while explaining what requires revision. They should also allow employees to ask questions and provide relevant context. The approach demonstrated by George Meyer shows that demanding standards and concern for colleagues do not have to conflict.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare feedback for an employee whose work contains a significant error and include the specific issue, impact, corrective action, and support required."
Key point:
- Effective criticism focuses on observable work and actionable improvements.
- Respectful feedback protects participation without compromising quality.
Source links:
- Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Administrative Science Quarterly
27. Q: Why is emotional support important when employees receive criticism or experience revisions to their work?
A: Show answer
People may become emotionally attached to projects because they invest time, effort, professional judgment, and personal creativity in them. When someone rejects or substantially changes their work, they may interpret the decision as a rejection of their competence. Emotional support helps employees process difficult feedback, distinguish professional disagreement from personal rejection, and return their attention to the shared objective. In the chapter, George Meyer recognized that colleagues could become distressed when their scripts were rewritten. He provided support while remaining honest about the quality of their work. In organizational settings, managers can use private discussions, clear explanations, listening, and practical guidance to reduce unnecessary defensiveness. Emotional support should complement professional review, not replace accurate decisions or necessary corrective action.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a response for a colleague whose proposal has been rejected and explain how to preserve motivation while maintaining the required standard."
Key point:
- Emotional support can make difficult feedback easier to accept.
- Concern for employees should not prevent necessary quality improvements.
Source links:
- Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Administrative Science Quarterly
28. Q: What is the perspective gap, and how does it affect workplace relationships?
A: Show answer
The perspective gap is the difficulty of accurately understanding how another person experiences a situation when we are not experiencing the same emotional or physical state. People often underestimate how strongly another person's current circumstances influence decisions and reactions. The chapter illustrates this through research in which individuals who had recently experienced cold could not accurately remember or estimate its intensity after returning to a comfortable state. In workplaces, managers may underestimate the effect of workload pressure, public criticism, uncertainty, or disappointing feedback on employees. They may therefore assume that their own preferred response will be acceptable to everyone else. Reducing the perspective gap requires active listening, direct questions, consideration of context, and attention to the experiences of the person affected.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a workplace disagreement and explain how the emotional state of each participant may influence the interpretation of the situation."
Key point:
- People may underestimate experiences they are not currently feeling.
- Understanding context requires more than assuming how another person would react in the same situation.
Source links:
- Visceral Drives in Retrospect: Explanations About the Inaccessible Past — Psychological Science
29. Q: Why is relying on personal experience insufficient when trying to understand another person's feelings?
A: Show answer
Personal experience provides useful information but does not automatically reveal another person's preferences, circumstances, or emotional reactions. Individuals tend to use their own experiences as a reference point, assuming that others will respond similarly. This can lead to inaccurate judgments about what support is needed, how feedback should be delivered, or which decisions will be appreciated. A manager who prefers public recognition may assume that every employee wants the same treatment, while an employee who values independent work may underestimate a colleague's need for assistance. Effective perspective-taking requires examining the other person's actual situation rather than merely imagining how the decision would affect oneself. Direct communication and observation help replace assumptions with more reliable understanding.
Study instruction:
- "List three situations in which your own preferences may differ from those of a colleague, client, supervisor, or beneficiary."
Key point:
- Personal preferences should not be treated as universal preferences.
- Understanding others requires gathering information about their actual needs.
Source links:
- Give Them What They Want: The Benefits of Explicitness in Gift Exchange — Stanford Graduate School of Business
30. Q: What is the difference between perspective-taking and projecting one's own preferences onto another person?
A: Show answer
Perspective-taking attempts to understand another person's preferences and circumstances from that person's position. Projection occurs when someone assumes that others want what they themselves would want in the same situation. The difference is important because projection can appear considerate while producing an unhelpful result. The chapter's gift-giving example demonstrates that people may believe an unusual personal choice is more thoughtful than selecting something specifically requested by the recipient. The giver is evaluating the decision through personal preferences instead of the recipient's stated preferences. In professional relationships, projection can occur when managers impose communication styles, training methods, incentives, or solutions without understanding what employees actually need. Good perspective-taking involves asking, listening, testing assumptions, and adjusting the response accordingly.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a practical decision in which someone projects personal preferences and redesign the decision using the affected person's actual needs."
Key point:
- Projection asks what would satisfy the decision-maker.
- Perspective-taking investigates what would genuinely help the recipient.
Source links:
- Give Them What They Want: The Benefits of Explicitness in Gift Exchange — Stanford Graduate School of Business
31. Q: Why can a requested gift be more appreciated than an unexpected gift?
A: Show answer
People giving gifts often believe that selecting an unexpected item demonstrates additional thought, creativity, and personal effort. However, recipients may value a requested item more because it reflects their actual needs and preferences. Research by Francesca Gino and Frank Flynn, discussed in the chapter, found that gift recipients appreciated requested gifts more than givers expected. The underlying principle applies beyond personal gifts. A manager providing training, a supervisor assigning support, or a colleague offering assistance should consider whether the recipient actually wants or needs the proposed intervention. The effort invested by the giver does not guarantee that the recipient will find the action useful. A thoughtful contribution should be evaluated from the recipient's perspective rather than solely through the giver's intentions.
Study instruction:
- "Compare an unsolicited solution with a solution explicitly requested by a colleague and explain which information should guide the decision."
Key point:
- Good intentions do not guarantee that a contribution will meet another person's needs.
- Requested assistance can be more valuable than an unsolicited alternative.
Source links:
- Give Them What They Want: The Benefits of Explicitness in Gift Exchange — Journal of Experimental Social Psychology
32. Q: How can employees identify what colleagues genuinely need before offering assistance?
A: Show answer
Employees should begin by clarifying the problem rather than immediately proposing a solution. Useful questions include what has already been attempted, which part of the task is most difficult, what outcome is required, what resources are unavailable, and whether the individual wants advice, practical assistance, or simply time to explain the issue. The person offering help should also consider urgency, confidentiality, workload, and the recipient's authority to make decisions. For example, a finance officer requesting assistance with a donor reconciliation may need additional source documents rather than someone taking over the reconciliation. Asking first prevents unnecessary intervention and respects the recipient's professional judgment. The assistance should then be checked against the intended outcome to confirm that it was useful.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare five open-ended questions for identifying another person's needs before providing assistance."
Key point:
- Effective assistance begins with understanding the actual problem.
- Ask what support is needed rather than assuming the answer.
Source links:
- Give Them What They Want: The Benefits of Explicitness in Gift Exchange — Stanford Graduate School of Business
33. Q: What does the children's food-sharing experiment illustrate about the development of perspective-taking?
A: Show answer
The chapter describes an experiment in which young children were offered two foods with different personal appeal. They then observed a researcher expressing dislike for one food and preference for the other. When asked to share food, the children had to decide whether to offer what they personally preferred or what the researcher preferred. The findings illustrated developmental differences in the ability to recognize that another person's preferences can differ from one's own. The professional implication is that perspective-taking involves recognizing another person's independent viewpoint rather than automatically applying personal preferences. However, a developmental experiment should not be interpreted as a complete explanation of adult workplace behavior. Perspective-taking also requires practice, communication, contextual knowledge, and willingness to revise assumptions.
Study instruction:
- "Explain why another person's preferences may differ from your own and identify practical methods for checking those preferences in professional situations."
Key point:
- Perspective-taking requires recognizing that different people can prefer different outcomes.
- Understanding another person's perspective is a skill that can be strengthened through practice.
34. Q: Why is a professional reputation especially valuable in industries with closely connected networks?
A: Show answer
In industries where people repeatedly work with the same colleagues, clients, and professional contacts, reputation influences future opportunities. A person known for reliability, constructive assistance, and fairness may receive recommendations and invitations to participate in new projects. Someone who repeatedly undermines colleagues or claims disproportionate credit may find that relationships become less available. The chapter uses George Meyer's experience in television comedy to illustrate how professional networks can function as a relatively small and interconnected community. His decision to see other writers as allies rather than permanent competitors helped him develop lasting relationships. Reputation does not replace technical quality, but it can influence whether others are willing to collaborate and recommend an individual.
Study instruction:
- "Map a professional network and identify how recommendations, past collaboration, reliability, and interpersonal conduct affect access to future opportunities."
Key point:
- Professional relationships can influence recruitment, referrals, and collaboration opportunities.
- Reputation is built through repeated behavior rather than occasional self-promotion.
35. Q: Why should colleagues sometimes be viewed as allies rather than competitors?
A: Show answer
Employees may compete for limited positions, recognition, budgets, or leadership opportunities, but this does not mean that every professional relationship is a zero-sum contest. Colleagues can share information, improve each other's work, strengthen industry standards, and create opportunities that benefit multiple participants. In the chapter, George Meyer recognized that the television comedy industry was a connected community in which the success of other writers could contribute positively to the wider profession. This change in perspective helped him replace jealousy with genuine support for colleagues. Organizations can encourage a similar mindset by defining shared goals, recognizing collective achievements, and rewarding constructive collaboration alongside individual performance. Healthy competition remains possible, but it should not undermine relationships or prevent knowledge exchange.
Study instruction:
- "Identify a professional situation involving competition and propose ways to preserve cooperation while maintaining fair individual recognition."
Key point:
- Colleagues can be collaborators even when professional opportunities are limited.
- Healthy competition should not encourage unnecessary hostility or information hiding.
Source links:
- What Goes Around Comes Around: Knowledge Hiding, Perceived Motivational Climate, and Creativity — Academy of Management Journal
36. Q: How does generous behavior create a positive ripple effect beyond the immediate recipient?
A: Show answer
A positive ripple effect occurs when assistance received by one person improves that person's ability or willingness to help others. A colleague who receives constructive mentoring may subsequently support junior employees. An employee who learns to provide clear feedback may improve the working relationships of an entire department. George Meyer's contributions to Army Man and The Simpsons demonstrate how an individual can support contributors whose later careers generate further opportunities for others. The effect can therefore extend beyond the original relationship and continue through professional networks. However, the ripple effect is not automatic. Organizations need environments in which people can apply what they learn, share opportunities, and develop new relationships. Leaders can strengthen it by recognizing mentoring, collaborative achievements, and knowledge transfer.
Study instruction:
- "Trace how one act of professional assistance could create subsequent benefits for other individuals, teams, and organizational activities."
Key point:
- The benefits of giving can extend through networks and future relationships.
- Mentoring and knowledge sharing help transmit beneficial behavior across teams.
37. Q: What should a manager assess before recruiting a highly successful employee from another organization?
A: Show answer
A manager should examine the employee's individual expertise, previous results, working relationships, available resources, organizational fit, and ability to operate in a different environment. It is important to distinguish achievements produced mainly through personal capability from those supported by specialized teams or organizational infrastructure. Managers should identify which colleagues, systems, relationships, and information sources were essential to the employee's previous performance. Recruitment planning should also include onboarding, relationship development, access to necessary resources, and realistic performance expectations during the transition. Where the original team is important, the organization should evaluate whether recruiting several complementary professionals would create greater value than hiring one individual in isolation. The decision should be based on evidence about how performance is produced, not simply on reputation or previous rankings.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a recruitment assessment framework that evaluates individual talent, organizational fit, team dependencies, and the conditions needed to sustain performance."
Key point:
- Hiring individual talent does not automatically transfer the previous team's capabilities.
- Recruitment decisions should examine the context supporting earlier achievements.
Source links:
- Can They Take It With Them? — Management Science
- The Firm Specificity of Individual Performance — Management Science
38. Q: How should a manager respond when a team member complains that another colleague has taken credit for their work?
A: Show answer
The manager should first establish the facts rather than immediately taking sides. Review the original assignment, documented responsibilities, work products, communications, meeting records, and the sequence of contributions. Ask each person to explain their role, including idea development, technical review, implementation, coordination, and final approval. The manager should distinguish genuine shared contributions from inaccurate attribution and recognize whether the problem arose from unclear expectations or deliberate misrepresentation. Once the facts are established, correct the recognition record, communicate appropriate acknowledgment, and address any behavior that violates organizational expectations. For recurring problems, improve contribution documentation and review procedures. The objective is to restore fairness and trust while preventing future disputes.
Study instruction:
- "Develop an investigation checklist for a credit dispute and identify the evidence needed before making a management decision."
Key point:
- Credit disputes require evidence-based investigation.
- Clear contribution records reduce repeated disagreements.
Source links:
- Egocentric Biases in Availability and Attribution — Journal of Personality and Social Psychology
39. Q: What should a manager do when an employee repeatedly withholds information from colleagues?
A: Show answer
The manager should determine whether the information is legitimately confidential, restricted by policy, or unnecessarily withheld. Employees may have valid reasons not to disclose personal data, commercially sensitive information, or protected organizational information. However, hiding routine knowledge needed for colleagues to perform their responsibilities can damage cooperation. Managers should establish clear information-sharing expectations, investigate whether performance incentives encourage internal competition, and determine whether workload, distrust, or unclear responsibilities contribute to the behavior. Where inappropriate knowledge hiding is established, provide direct feedback and require corrective action. Repeated behavior may need formal performance management. The organization should also make essential information accessible through approved systems and procedures.
Study instruction:
- "Classify examples of withheld information and design an appropriate management response for each case."
Key point:
- Legitimate confidentiality must be distinguished from harmful knowledge hiding.
- Information-sharing expectations should be clear and supported by organizational processes.
Source links:
- What Goes Around Comes Around: Knowledge Hiding, Perceived Motivational Climate, and Creativity — Academy of Management Journal
40. Q: How can an organization encourage giving without allowing generous employees to become overloaded or exploited?
A: Show answer
Encouraging generosity should not mean expecting employees to accept unlimited work or continuously sacrifice their own responsibilities. A healthy giving culture recognizes contributions, establishes workload boundaries, provides appropriate resources, and prevents assistance from becoming an excuse for poor accountability elsewhere. Managers should monitor whether the same employees consistently perform unpopular assignments, provide informal mentoring, or rescue colleagues from preventable problems. They should distribute assistance fairly, establish escalation procedures, and recognize collaborative work in performance assessments. Employees should also be able to decline or renegotiate requests when workload or responsibility limits are exceeded. This distinction is important because sustainable giving requires organizational fairness, not permanent self-sacrifice. Generosity is most useful when it strengthens collective performance without undermining the contributor's well-being or responsibilities.
Study instruction:
- "Review a team's workload distribution and identify whether helping behavior is being recognized, shared fairly, and supported by appropriate boundaries."
Key point:
- Generosity must coexist with fair workload allocation and personal accountability.
- Sustainable collaboration should strengthen contributors rather than exhaust them.
Source links:
- Expedition Behavior — National Outdoor Leadership School
41. Q: How can leaders develop a culture in which employees support one another's professional development?
A: Show answer
Leaders can establish expectations for mentoring, knowledge sharing, constructive feedback, and recognition of collaborative contributions. They should model these behaviors by acknowledging others' work, asking for advice, admitting mistakes, and supporting employees who raise valid concerns. Development activities should create opportunities for experienced employees to transfer knowledge to less experienced colleagues through practical assignments, coaching, and structured reviews. Performance assessments should recognize not only individual output but also activities that strengthen team capabilities. Managers should also monitor whether collaboration is genuinely useful rather than simply measuring the number of meetings or mentoring sessions. A successful culture is demonstrated through improved knowledge access, stronger working relationships, better problem-solving, and increased employee capability.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a leadership development plan that incorporates mentoring, knowledge sharing, constructive feedback, and collaborative performance indicators."
Key point:
- Leaders influence collaboration through both expectations and personal example.
- Mentoring becomes valuable when it develops practical employee capabilities.
Source links:
- Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Administrative Science Quarterly
42. Q: How can psychological safety be measured or evaluated in an organization?
A: Show answer
Psychological safety can be assessed through employee perceptions and observable team behaviors. Organizations can ask whether employees feel comfortable raising concerns, asking questions, reporting errors, challenging decisions, and proposing improvements. These survey findings should be supplemented by operational evidence, such as participation in discussions, quality of incident reporting, response to mistakes, and whether management acts constructively on employee feedback. Results should be interpreted carefully because a team that reports few problems may either have strong processes or may feel uncomfortable disclosing issues. High reporting levels can reflect a healthy willingness to speak up rather than poor performance. Managers should compare survey trends with actual learning activities and team outcomes. Assessment should lead to improvement actions, not simply produce a numerical score.
Study instruction:
- "Design a short psychological safety assessment and specify how survey results will be compared with actual team behaviors."
Key point:
- Psychological safety should be evaluated through perceptions and observable behavior.
- Reporting and speaking-up patterns require contextual interpretation.
Source links:
- Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Administrative Science Quarterly
43. Q: How can an organization recognize the contributions of individuals without weakening collective ownership of results?
A: Show answer
An organization should define clear recognition criteria that acknowledge significant individual work while preserving the importance of shared achievements. For major projects, documentation can identify the project sponsor, technical contributors, reviewers, implementation team, and decision-makers. Managers should distinguish between individual intellectual contributions, team coordination, operational execution, and organizational resources. Recognition may take different forms, including formal acknowledgment, professional development opportunities, performance evaluation, authorship, awards, and promotion decisions. The specific method should reflect the nature of the achievement and the applicable organizational policy. Clear recognition systems reduce unnecessary disputes because employees do not need to choose between protecting their contribution and supporting their colleagues. Collective ownership should not erase individual responsibility, while individual recognition should not obscure the work of others.
Study instruction:
- "Create a recognition framework that distinguishes individual responsibility, shared contribution, technical leadership, and team achievement."
Key point:
- Individual recognition and collective achievement can be acknowledged together.
- Transparent attribution reduces disputes and encourages continued collaboration.
44. Q: How should organizations handle conflicts between personal ambition and collective objectives?
A: Show answer
Personal ambition is not inherently harmful because individuals need opportunities for development, recognition, and career progression. Problems emerge when employees pursue these objectives through behavior that undermines colleagues or damages the team's ability to perform. Managers should clarify shared goals, establish fair evaluation criteria, identify legitimate individual development opportunities, and address behavior that discourages cooperation. Employees should be encouraged to pursue excellent individual performance while recognizing that many results depend on collective capabilities. When a conflict arises, decision-makers should examine whether the disagreement concerns genuinely limited resources, unclear responsibilities, or unnecessary competition. The aim is not to remove ambition but to align it with sustainable team performance and organizational objectives.
Study instruction:
- "Analyze a workplace conflict involving personal advancement and collective responsibility, then propose an approach that protects both fairness and team effectiveness."
Key point:
- Personal ambition can coexist with concern for collective success.
- Organizational incentives should not reward achievement at the expense of collaboration.
45. Q: What practical habits can employees adopt to become more effective collaborators?
A: Show answer
Employees can develop collaboration through repeated, observable behaviors. These include sharing useful information, giving specific feedback, acknowledging other people's contributions, asking colleagues what support they need, accepting responsibility for mistakes, and volunteering for necessary work when appropriate. They should avoid creating unnecessary dependency, withholding information for personal advantage, or treating every professional interaction as a competition. Employees can also strengthen perspective-taking by asking questions before making assumptions and considering the workload and preferences of the people affected by their decisions. Another useful habit is maintaining a record of collective progress and individual responsibilities to improve accountability. These behaviors are more effective when applied consistently rather than used selectively when public recognition is available.
Study instruction:
- "Select five collaboration habits and convert each into an observable weekly workplace action."
Key point:
- Collaboration improves through consistent everyday behavior.
- Useful assistance combines generosity, awareness, and accountability.
Source links:
- Expedition Behavior — National Outdoor Leadership School
46. Q: How can managers improve collaboration in a creative team that frequently disagrees about ideas and revisions?
A: Show answer
Managers should establish a process in which ideas can be proposed, discussed, tested, and improved without personal humiliation. Team members should understand the creative objective, decision-making authority, quality criteria, and responsibilities for revisions. Feedback should identify specific strengths and weaknesses rather than rely on personal judgments. Managers can also rotate review responsibilities, encourage less experienced employees to contribute, and recognize people who improve other colleagues' work. Disagreement should remain focused on the merits of the ideas and the available evidence. When a proposal is rejected, the reason should be explained sufficiently for the author to understand the decision and learn from it. This approach combines creative freedom with clear standards and accountability for the final result.
Study instruction:
- "Design a creative review workflow with proposal submission, peer feedback, revision, decision-making, and recognition of contributors."
Key point:
- Constructive disagreement can improve creative output.
- Clear review procedures prevent criticism from becoming personal conflict.
Source links:
- Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Administrative Science Quarterly
47. Q: How can organizations prevent talented employees from becoming isolated from important collaborators?
A: Show answer
Organizations should encourage cross-functional relationships, mentoring, project rotations, peer review, and participation in professional communities. High-performing employees should be connected to colleagues whose expertise complements their own. Managers should also recognize when employees are becoming excessively dependent on a single relationship or when institutional knowledge is concentrated in one individual. Regular knowledge-sharing activities and documented work processes can reduce these risks. However, organizations should avoid forcing unnecessary interactions that distract employees from important technical work. The objective is to create meaningful professional relationships that improve access to information and support effective execution. Performance should be understood as the combined result of personal expertise and the relationships that enable expertise to be applied.
Study instruction:
- "Map key professional relationships within a team and identify areas where expertise is isolated or collaboration is insufficient."
Key point:
- Strong professional networks help employees access complementary expertise.
- Knowledge concentration and isolation create organizational continuity risks.
Source links:
- The Firm Specificity of Individual Performance — Management Science
48. Q: What is the relationship between trust, knowledge sharing, and long-term team effectiveness?
A: Show answer
Trust influences whether people feel comfortable sharing information, asking for assistance, acknowledging uncertainty, and relying on colleagues. When employees believe that their contributions will be treated fairly, they may be more willing to exchange knowledge and participate in collective problem-solving. Conversely, repeated experiences of credit appropriation, disrespect, or knowledge hiding can make employees reluctant to cooperate. The chapter illustrates how George Meyer's behavior helped create an environment in which colleagues trusted him with unfinished work and valued his feedback. Trust should not be based solely on personal goodwill. Organizations also need transparent responsibilities, confidentiality rules, fair recognition, and reliable processes. These structures make cooperative relationships more sustainable, especially when teams work under pressure.
Study instruction:
- "Identify the behaviors and organizational procedures that increase or reduce trust in a team."
Key point:
- Trust supports knowledge exchange and constructive professional relationships.
- Fair procedures reinforce trust by making collaboration predictable and accountable.
Source links:
- What Goes Around Comes Around: Knowledge Hiding, Perceived Motivational Climate, and Creativity — Academy of Management Journal
- Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Administrative Science Quarterly
49. Q: How should a team evaluate whether collaboration has actually improved performance?
A: Show answer
Collaboration should be evaluated through both relationship indicators and operational outcomes. Relationship indicators may include participation, knowledge sharing, mentoring, feedback quality, and trust. Operational indicators may include project completion, work quality, innovation, error reduction, customer or beneficiary satisfaction, and the reliability of deliverables. Managers should avoid relying on the number of meetings or messages exchanged because high communication volume does not necessarily indicate effective collaboration. Evaluations should also consider whether the group has become more capable of completing work without excessive dependence on a single person. Where possible, compare results over time and examine whether improvements can reasonably be linked to changes in collaboration practices. The purpose is to identify which behaviors produce meaningful results and which processes require adjustment.
Study instruction:
- "Develop a collaboration scorecard containing at least three behavior indicators and three operational performance indicators."
Key point:
- Collaboration should be measured through behaviors and actual outcomes.
- More communication does not necessarily mean better performance.
Source links:
- Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Administrative Science Quarterly
50. Q: What are the main lessons organizations should apply from the relationship between giving, collaboration, creativity, and shared credit?
A: Show answer
Organizations should recognize that professional performance is produced through a combination of individual ability, institutional resources, and working relationships. Employees can strengthen collective success by sharing knowledge, supporting colleagues, giving accurate recognition, accepting responsibility, and considering the needs of people affected by their decisions. Managers should foster psychological safety while maintaining high standards and clear accountability. They should also reduce responsibility bias, prevent knowledge hiding, and establish fair procedures for recognizing contributions. George Meyer demonstrates how an individual's creative ability can generate wider benefits when used to develop colleagues. Frank Lloyd Wright and Jonas Salk illustrate the risks of underestimating collaboration and failing to acknowledge other people's contributions. The broader lesson is that sustainable success is not simply about becoming the most visible person in a group. It is also about strengthening the people, relationships, and institutions that make achievement possible.
Study instruction:
- "Prepare a personal and organizational action plan covering knowledge sharing, perspective-taking, psychological safety, fair recognition, and collective performance."
Key point:
- Sustainable professional success requires both individual capability and constructive collaboration.
- The strongest contributors help create conditions in which other people can succeed as well.
Source links:
- Psychological Safety and Learning Behavior in Work Teams — Administrative Science Quarterly
- What Goes Around Comes Around: Knowledge Hiding, Perceived Motivational Climate, and Creativity — Academy of Management Journal

Table 4
Recognizing and Developing Human Potential
1. Q: What is the central message of recognizing and developing potential in others?
A: Show answer
Recognizing potential means looking beyond a person's current performance and considering what that person could achieve with appropriate encouragement, learning opportunities, guidance, and sustained effort. The chapter illustrates that individuals who eventually become outstanding performers do not necessarily display exceptional ability at the beginning. Their development is often influenced by teachers, managers, coaches, and mentors who recognize promising qualities that others overlook. The central lesson is that identifying talent and developing talent are interconnected responsibilities. A person should not be judged exclusively by current results when motivation, perseverance, learning capacity, and responsiveness to guidance suggest room for substantial improvement. Leaders can create conditions that help people turn previously unnoticed capabilities into meaningful performance.

Study instruction:
- Explain the difference between recognizing existing talent and developing future potential. Identify three workplace examples where someone could achieve more with appropriate support.
Key point:
- Current performance does not always reveal a person's full potential.
- Development opportunities can influence future performance.
Source links:
- Self-Fulfilling Prophecy as a Management Tool — Academy of Management Review
2. Q: What is the difference between talent, potential, motivation, and demonstrated performance?
A: Show answer
Talent refers to capabilities that can help someone perform successfully in a particular area. Potential concerns the capacity to develop further, sometimes beyond what present performance indicates. Motivation represents the willingness to invest effort in learning and improvement, while demonstrated performance represents the results actually achieved under particular circumstances. These concepts are related but should not be treated as interchangeable. A talented individual may lack motivation, while a highly motivated individual may still need foundational skills or technical development. Someone with modest initial performance may eventually become highly capable through sustained practice and effective mentoring. A reliable talent assessment therefore examines current competence, learning progress, motivation, relevant personal qualities, and the opportunities available to demonstrate improvement.

Study instruction:
- Select three employees or hypothetical candidates and distinguish their current competence, motivation, development potential, and demonstrated results.
Key point:
- Talent, potential, motivation, and performance measure different dimensions.
- Development decisions should consider both present evidence and future learning capacity.
Source links:
- Talent Development Research — Benjamin Bloom, Educational Leadership
3. Q: Why can conventional talent assessment overlook people with substantial future potential?
A: Show answer
Conventional assessment frequently emphasizes visible achievements, qualifications, technical ability, previous experience, and established performance records. These indicators are useful, but they can disadvantage candidates who have not yet received sufficient opportunities to demonstrate their abilities.
